English for Work · Customers & creative work

Nail Technician English

Clarify nail-service requests, compare shapes and finishes, agree on designs and prices, respond to discomfort, and coordinate appointments and service reviews.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Read the situation.
Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For Nail technicians, manicurists, pedicurists, and nail-salon reception staff.

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Clarify the desired length before treating an ambiguous service label or reference photograph as an instruction.
  • Compare sample silhouettes and lengths while preserving a practical requirement and avoiding unsupported durability claims.
  • Separate color, coverage, and finish while leaving an unspecified product system open for consultation.
  • Calculate a per-nail addition, offer complete priced alternatives, and confirm quantity and placement before starting.
  • Pause a cosmetic request, preserve the client report, and distinguish healthcare referral from salon service approval.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Your eight lessons

Open the learner book (PDF) ↗
01 Clarifying the service and the intended length02 Comparing shape and practical length03 Explaining color, coverage, and finish04 Agreeing on nail art and a complete quote05 Responding to discomfort and uncertain nail concerns06 Giving a clear station-readiness update07 Handling a chip or design concern08 Scheduling follow-up and handing over exact requests
Prompts

All clients, salons, prices, sample descriptions, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not nail procedures, product removal, disinfection methods, medical diagnosis, treatment, or legal requirements. Follow professional training, current product instructions, actual salon procedures, applicable local rules, and emergency arrangements. Health concerns belong with an appropriate healthcare professional; cosmetic-service suitability requires its own salon assessment. No example authorizes covering an unexplained concern with product.

01Clarifying the service and the intended lengthThe picture is long; the request is short+

Your goal

Clarify the desired length before treating an ambiguous service label or reference photograph as an instruction.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

The picture is long; the request is short

Client Zoe booked an overlay but calls the appointment a full set when speaking to technician Lin. Zoe points to a long reference image, then explains that the current short length should be kept. No work has begun. Lin clarifies that the intended discussion is a natural-nail overlay without adding length, not automatically extensions. The image is a reference for the look, not permission to copy its length. Product choice, any further design details, and suitability still need the normal consultation.

Zoe: Client / Lin: Nail technician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What length does Zoe want?

Answer and explanation

B. The current short length retained

Zoe explicitly wants the existing short length rather than the long reference.

2. What was booked?

Answer and explanation

B. An overlay

The booking says overlay even though Zoe uses another label in conversation.

3. What does the reference image authorize by itself?

Answer and explanation

D. No automatic length change

An image starts the discussion but does not replace explicit agreement about length.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Overlay is a broad coating term, and full set can be used loosely. In this conversation, the client wants a natural-nail overlay at the current short length, with no extensions requested. Confirm the result before relying on either label.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
natural nail
Client's own nail rather than an added enhancement.identify the natural nail
nail plate
Hard visible part of the natural nail.refer to the nail plate
nail bed
Tissue beneath the nail plate.distinguish nail bed and plate
free edge
Part of the nail extending beyond its attachment at the fingertip.discuss the free edge
overlay
Coating over a nail surface; the exact service needs clarification.clarify the overlay
natural-nail overlay
Coating applied over the existing natural nail without necessarily adding length.discuss a natural-nail overlay
extension
Added length beyond the existing natural nail.confirm whether extensions are wanted
enhancement
Added material used to change nail appearance or structure.clarify the enhancement
full set
Common service label whose intended scope must be confirmed.clarify the full-set request
tip
Preformed component used in some nail-extension services.identify an extension tip
form
Temporary support used in some sculpted-enhancement services.distinguish a form from a tip
builder gel
Gel product category used to create enhancement structure.discuss builder gel terminology
acrylic
Common salon term for liquid-and-powder enhancement systems.clarify the acrylic service
gel polish
Light-cured color-coating category, distinct from all enhancement services.distinguish gel polish
lacquer
Conventional nail-polish category, distinct from gel polish.identify nail lacquer
service label
Name used on a booking or menu.clarify the service label
reference image
Picture used to discuss selected visual features.interpret the reference image
retained length
Existing length the client wants to keep.confirm retained length
added length
Extra length created by an extension service.decline added length
current length
Length present before the proposed service begins.check the current length
length preference
Client's stated choice about how long the result should be.record the length preference
consultation
Discussion of the request, suitability, and proposed service.complete the consultation
service scope
Specific work included in the agreed appointment.confirm the service scope
read-back
Repetition of key details to verify understanding.give a length read-back

A gel-polish color service and a structural enhancement are not interchangeable labels. The desired short length does not select a product system or establish suitability. Keep those decisions within the actual consultation and professional process.

C Notice the language

Check the meaning without correcting the person

Clients often learn service words from friends, menus, or social media, where the same label may be used differently. Ask what result they want, explain the relevant distinction plainly, and avoid making the client feel that knowing the salon vocabulary was a condition of asking.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the label
When you say full set, do you mean added length?
Ask about the result
Would you like to keep your current length?
Refer to the booking
Your booking currently says overlay.
Separate image and instruction
The photo shows long nails, but we should confirm your own length preference.
Confirm the practical choice
You want your current short length retained.
Explain the proposed term
We are discussing a natural-nail overlay without added length.
Keep extensions separate
No extensions are requested at this stage.
Avoid a product assumption
We have not selected the product system yet.
Check the visual feature
Which part of the image do you want to use as a reference?
Confirm before starting
No work has begun, so let us settle the details first.
Avoid embarrassment
Service names can be used differently; the result you want is what matters.
Distinguish scope
Adding length would be a different part of the service discussion.
Invite correction
Please correct me if short does not describe what you mean.
Keep assessment open
Suitability still needs the normal consultation.
Read back the request
Current short length, natural-nail overlay discussion, no extensions.
Close the clarification
The picture does not override your confirmed length preference.
6 language and precision notes
Keep versus add
Keep preserves the existing length; add proposes an extension.
Full set
Confirm what the client means instead of treating the label as self-explanatory.
Natural-nail overlay
This specifies the surface and avoids assuming the overlay includes an extension tip.
Reference only
An image may illustrate one feature without authorizing all of them.
At this stage
The current request is clear while product and suitability decisions remain open.
Read-back
Repeating the agreed length catches a consequential misunderstanding before work starts.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question resolves the key ambiguity?

Answer and explanation

B. Do you want added length, or your current short length kept?

The question directly checks the client's actual length preference rather than assuming the label.

2. Which summary is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Natural-nail overlay discussion at current short length, with no extensions requested.

The summary preserves the clarified length and leaves other service decisions open.

3. Which product statement is supported?

Answer and explanation

D. Product selection still needs clarification.

The case does not select a system or bypass the normal consultation.

4. Which response avoids blaming the client for terminology?

Answer and explanation

C. Service names vary; let us confirm the result you want.

The response invites clarification respectfully instead of treating vocabulary knowledge as a prerequisite.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

The picture is long; the request is short

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Zoe: Client / Lin: Nail technician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Extensions
  • product system
  • service label
  • nail plate
  • Builder gel
  • retained length
  • overlay
  • length preference
  • read-back
  • reference image
  1. Zoe
    I'm here for a full set. I brought this picture, but please don't make them this long. I want to keep my own length.
  2. Lin
    Your booking says 1. Do you want material over your existing nails, without adding length beyond them?
  3. Zoe
    Yes, that's what I meant. I heard someone call it a full set and assumed that was the right name.
  4. Lin
    That's fine. The 2 can be used differently. Your short-length preference is more useful than guessing from the name.
  5. Zoe
    Good. I like the look in the photograph, but the long nails would get in my way at work.
  6. Lin
    We'll use the 3 to discuss the look, not copy every feature. No extra length is requested.
  7. Zoe
    So an overlay and extensions aren't the same thing? I'd like to know what the menu means next time.
  8. Lin
    4 add length. Here we're discussing material over the nail you already have, without adding to it.
  9. Zoe
    And the hard part I can see is the nail plate? I've heard that called the nail bed too.
  10. Lin
    The 5 is the hard visible part. The nail bed is the tissue underneath, so those terms aren't interchangeable.
  11. Zoe
    That helps. What about builder gel and gel polish? I thought anything with gel in the name was the same service.
  12. Lin
    6 is used for enhancement structure. Gel polish is a color-coating category. We'll discuss which service is appropriate rather than treat them as synonyms.
  13. Zoe
    I haven't chosen a material yet. I'd like you to explain the options before we settle on one.
  14. Lin
    Certainly. The 7 is still open. Keeping the nails short doesn't answer that question by itself.
  15. Zoe
    Please keep that short-length request on the booking. I don't want the picture confusing anyone else who helps today.
  16. Lin
    I'll record your 8 clearly, along with no extensions requested. We can then continue with the other design details.
  17. Zoe
    Could you repeat the main point before we start? I want to be sure I explained it properly.
  18. Lin
    Here's the 9: natural-nail overlay discussion, current short length kept, no extensions. Product and suitability still need consultation.
  19. Zoe
    Yes, that's right. I want the look discussed, not the long length copied.
  20. Lin
    Understood. That's the 10 we'll work from. Nothing has begun, so we can clarify the remaining choices first.

Answers and explanations
  1. overlay Turn 2. Overlay is the booked service; the word alone does not authorize extensions or choose a product.
  2. service label Turn 4. A service label names an appointment, but checking the desired result resolves differences in how people use it.
  3. reference image Turn 6. The image supplies visual ideas but does not replace Zoe's explicit decision to retain short nails.
  4. Extensions Turn 8. Extensions add length beyond the existing natural nail, which Zoe has declined in this case.
  5. nail plate Turn 10. The plate is the hard visible nail; the nail bed is the tissue beneath it.
  6. Builder gel Turn 12. Builder gel is used to create enhancement structure; gel polish names a color-coating category, not an identical service.
  7. product system Turn 14. The product system remains unselected even after the length preference has been clarified.
  8. retained length Turn 16. Retained length means the existing short length Zoe wants preserved rather than extended.
  9. read-back Turn 18. The read-back lets Zoe verify that her stated preference has not been replaced by the long reference.
  10. length preference Turn 20. The length preference is settled while other service choices remain open and no work has begun.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Zoe: I'm here for a full set. I brought this picture, but please don't make them this long. I want to keep my own length.
  2. Lin: Your booking says overlay. Do you want material over your existing nails, without adding length beyond them?
  3. Zoe: Yes, that's what I meant. I heard someone call it a full set and assumed that was the right name.
  4. Lin: That's fine. The service label can be used differently. Your short-length preference is more useful than guessing from the name.
  5. Zoe: Good. I like the look in the photograph, but the long nails would get in my way at work.
  6. Lin: We'll use the reference image to discuss the look, not copy every feature. No extra length is requested.
  7. Zoe: So an overlay and extensions aren't the same thing? I'd like to know what the menu means next time.
  8. Lin: Extensions add length. Here we're discussing material over the nail you already have, without adding to it.
  9. Zoe: And the hard part I can see is the nail plate? I've heard that called the nail bed too.
  10. Lin: The nail plate is the hard visible part. The nail bed is the tissue underneath, so those terms aren't interchangeable.
  11. Zoe: That helps. What about builder gel and gel polish? I thought anything with gel in the name was the same service.
  12. Lin: Builder gel is used for enhancement structure. Gel polish is a color-coating category. We'll discuss which service is appropriate rather than treat them as synonyms.
  13. Zoe: I haven't chosen a material yet. I'd like you to explain the options before we settle on one.
  14. Lin: Certainly. The product system is still open. Keeping the nails short doesn't answer that question by itself.
  15. Zoe: Please keep that short-length request on the booking. I don't want the picture confusing anyone else who helps today.
  16. Lin: I'll record your retained length clearly, along with no extensions requested. We can then continue with the other design details.
  17. Zoe: Could you repeat the main point before we start? I want to be sure I explained it properly.
  18. Lin: Here's the read-back: natural-nail overlay discussion, current short length kept, no extensions. Product and suitability still need consultation.
  19. Zoe: Yes, that's right. I want the look discussed, not the long length copied.
  20. Lin: Understood. That's the length preference we'll work from. Nothing has begun, so we can clarify the remaining choices first.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Long reference, short request

A nail technician clarifies an overlay booking.

  1. Technician: Your booking says overlay, but your photograph shows added length. Which part do you want?
  2. Client: Only the pink color. I need to keep my nails short for work.
  3. Technician: Then let's separate the color from the extra length shown in the reference picture.
  4. Client: I called it a full set on the phone. Was that the wrong expression?
  5. Technician: People use that phrase differently. Asking about actual length gives us a clearer shared request.
  6. Client: So an overlay doesn't mean you automatically make the nails as long as the photograph?
  7. Technician: That's right: it goes over the natural nail without deliberately adding length to it.
  8. Client: That's what I meant. I don't want extensions, even if they appear in the photograph.
  9. Technician: I'll note pink color requested, existing short length retained, and no extensions in today's request.
  10. Client: Good. Please show me the color sample separately so the long image doesn't confuse us.
Hands or feet on the booking?

Reception checks an unclear online service selection.

  1. Receptionist: Your booking lists a manicure, but your message mentions choosing a color for your toes.
  2. Client: I may have selected the wrong service. I wanted a pedicure for my feet.
  3. Receptionist: Thank you for checking. A manicure is for hands; a pedicure concerns feet and toenails.
  4. Client: Could you simply change the word, or does the appointment need a different time?
  5. Receptionist: I need to check the appropriate appointment space before confirming a different service.
  6. Client: I can come later if necessary. I don't need my hands done this week.
  7. Receptionist: There is a pedicure slot at three. That replaces the manicure rather than adding both.
  8. Client: Three works. Could the confirmation say pedicure so I can check the change?
  9. Receptionist: Yes, I've changed the booking to a pedicure at three and removed the manicure.
  10. Client: The new message matches what I wanted. I'll choose the toe color when I arrive.
One longer nail does not define the whole request

A client brings a reference showing mixed nail lengths.

  1. Technician: Tell me which length you want overall, rather than relying only on this reference photograph.
  2. Client: Most of the nails look short, but the middle one looks longer than mine.
  3. Technician: Are you requesting that extra length too, or just asking about the difference in the picture?
  4. Client: I'm asking about the difference. I want all of mine at my present short length.
  5. Technician: Then we won't treat the longer pictured nail as a request for an extension.
  6. Client: Could I still discuss the same color and simple pattern on my shorter nails?
  7. Technician: Yes, we can discuss their appearance separately from length, then assess the actual service request.
  8. Client: I don't want the booking changed to extensions just because I showed this image.
  9. Technician: Understood. Your request stays short length, with the color and pattern still to be chosen.
  10. Client: That's accurate. Let's look at shorter samples before I decide which pattern I prefer.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The picture is long; the request is short
  1. Read the booking clarification, stressing keep my own length and without adding length.
  2. Switch roles and read the plate-versus-bed and builder-gel-versus-gel-polish exchanges with the definitions intact.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify another overlay request

A client shows a long reference but wants the existing medium length retained. The discussion is a natural-nail overlay with no extensions requested. Product choice is still open, and no work has begun.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Technician: "The current ___ length should be retained."

Answer and explanation

D. medium

Medium is the existing length the client explicitly wants to keep.

2. Client: "No ___ are requested."

Answer and explanation

D. extensions

The long image does not change the client's no-added-length request.

3. Technician: "Product choice remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. open

A length preference does not select the product system or establish suitability.

4. Client: "No work has ___."

Answer and explanation

C. begun

Clarification takes place before the service starts in the supplied scenario.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Clarifying the service and the intended length
Original fictional case: Client Zoe booked an overlay but calls the appointment a full set when speaking to technician Lin. Zoe points to a long reference image, then explains that the current short length should be kept. No work has begun. Lin clarifies that the intended discussion is a natural-nail overlay without adding length, not automatically extensions. The image is a reference for the look, not permission to copy its length. Product choice, any further design details, and suitability still need the normal consultation.
Language workshop: The picture is long; the request is short
Communication goal: Clarify the desired length before treating an ambiguous service label or reference photograph as an instruction.
Language guidance: Overlay is a broad coating term, and full set can be used loosely. In this conversation, the client wants a natural-nail overlay at the current short length, with no extensions requested. Confirm the result before relying on either label.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): When you say full set, do you mean added length? / Would you like to keep your current length? / Your booking currently says overlay. / The photo shows long nails, but we should confirm your own length preference. / You want your current short length retained. / We are discussing a natural-nail overlay without added length. / No extensions are requested at this stage. / We have not selected the product system yet. / Which part of the image do you want to use as a reference? / No work has begun, so let us settle the details first. / Service names can be used differently; the result you want is what matters. / Adding length would be a different part of the service discussion. / Please correct me if short does not describe what you mean. / Suitability still needs the normal consultation. / Current short length, natural-nail overlay discussion, no extensions. / The picture does not override your confirmed length preference.
Vocabulary: natural nail: Client's own nail rather than an added enhancement. / nail plate: Hard visible part of the natural nail. / nail bed: Tissue beneath the nail plate. / free edge: Part of the nail extending beyond its attachment at the fingertip. / overlay: Coating over a nail surface; the exact service needs clarification. / natural-nail overlay: Coating applied over the existing natural nail without necessarily adding length. / extension: Added length beyond the existing natural nail. / enhancement: Added material used to change nail appearance or structure. / full set: Common service label whose intended scope must be confirmed. / tip: Preformed component used in some nail-extension services. / form: Temporary support used in some sculpted-enhancement services. / builder gel: Gel product category used to create enhancement structure. / acrylic: Common salon term for liquid-and-powder enhancement systems. / gel polish: Light-cured color-coating category, distinct from all enhancement services. / lacquer: Conventional nail-polish category, distinct from gel polish. / service label: Name used on a booking or menu. / reference image: Picture used to discuss selected visual features. / retained length: Existing length the client wants to keep. / added length: Extra length created by an extension service. / current length: Length present before the proposed service begins. / length preference: Client's stated choice about how long the result should be. / consultation: Discussion of the request, suitability, and proposed service. / service scope: Specific work included in the agreed appointment. / read-back: Repetition of key details to verify understanding.
Speaking task: Read the booking clarification, stressing keep my own length and without adding length. Switch roles and read the plate-versus-bed and builder-gel-versus-gel-polish exchanges with the definitions intact. Complete and check the transfer; retain medium length rather than the short length used in the main dialogue.
Partner: Zoe: Client / Lin: Nail technician

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Communication goal: Clarify the desired length before treating an ambiguous service label or reference photograph as an instruction.
Language guidance: Overlay is a broad coating term, and full set can be used loosely. In this conversation, the client wants a natural-nail overlay at the current short length, with no extensions requested. Confirm the result before relying on either label.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): When you say full set, do you mean added length? / Would you like to keep your current length? / Your booking currently says overlay. / The photo shows long nails, but we should confirm your own length preference. / You want your current short length retained. / We are discussing a natural-nail overlay without added length. / No extensions are requested at this stage. / We have not selected the product system yet. / Which part of the image do you want to use as a reference? / No work has begun, so let us settle the details first. / Service names can be used differently; the result you want is what matters. / Adding length would be a different part of the service discussion. / Please correct me if short does not describe what you mean. / Suitability still needs the normal consultation. / Current short length, natural-nail overlay discussion, no extensions. / The picture does not override your confirmed length preference.
Vocabulary: natural nail: Client's own nail rather than an added enhancement. / nail plate: Hard visible part of the natural nail. / nail bed: Tissue beneath the nail plate. / free edge: Part of the nail extending beyond its attachment at the fingertip. / overlay: Coating over a nail surface; the exact service needs clarification. / natural-nail overlay: Coating applied over the existing natural nail without necessarily adding length. / extension: Added length beyond the existing natural nail. / enhancement: Added material used to change nail appearance or structure. / full set: Common service label whose intended scope must be confirmed. / tip: Preformed component used in some nail-extension services. / form: Temporary support used in some sculpted-enhancement services. / builder gel: Gel product category used to create enhancement structure. / acrylic: Common salon term for liquid-and-powder enhancement systems. / gel polish: Light-cured color-coating category, distinct from all enhancement services. / lacquer: Conventional nail-polish category, distinct from gel polish. / service label: Name used on a booking or menu. / reference image: Picture used to discuss selected visual features. / retained length: Existing length the client wants to keep. / added length: Extra length created by an extension service. / current length: Length present before the proposed service begins. / length preference: Client's stated choice about how long the result should be. / consultation: Discussion of the request, suitability, and proposed service. / service scope: Specific work included in the agreed appointment. / read-back: Repetition of key details to verify understanding.
Speaking task: Read the booking clarification, stressing keep my own length and without adding length. Switch roles and read the plate-versus-bed and builder-gel-versus-gel-polish exchanges with the definitions intact. Complete and check the transfer; retain medium length rather than the short length used in the main dialogue.
Partner: Zoe: Client / Lin: Nail technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A client shows a long reference but wants the existing medium length retained. The discussion is a natural-nail overlay with no extensions requested. Product choice is still open, and no work has begun.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Zoe: Could you repeat the main point before we start? I want to be sure I explained it properly.
Lin: Here's the read-back: natural-nail overlay discussion, current short length kept, no extensions. Product and suitability still need consultation.
Zoe: Yes, that's right. I want the look discussed, not the long length copied.
Lin: Understood. That's the length preference we'll work from. Nothing has begun, so we can clarify the remaining choices first.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Lesson 01: Clarifying the service and the intended length
Original fictional case: Client Zoe booked an overlay but calls the appointment a full set when speaking to technician Lin. Zoe points to a long reference image, then explains that the current short length should be kept. No work has begun. Lin clarifies that the intended discussion is a natural-nail overlay without adding length, not automatically extensions. The image is a reference for the look, not permission to copy its length. Product choice, any further design details, and suitability still need the normal consultation.

02Comparing shape and practical lengthA shape that fits the working day+

Your goal

Compare sample silhouettes and lengths while preserving a practical requirement and avoiding unsupported durability claims.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

A shape that fits the working day

Client Amara likes a long almond sample but needs a short result for keyboard work. Technician Kai also has a short squoval sample, with a straighter free-edge outline and softened corners. The almond sample narrows toward a rounded tip and is visibly longer. Amara chooses the short squoval appearance for discussion. No method, exact measurement, or guarantee against breakage or discomfort is established. Kai must distinguish the visible comparison from a promise about how either shape will perform on the actual nails.

Amara: Client / Kai: Nail technician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What practical requirement does Amara state?

Answer and explanation

A. A short result for keyboard work

The client wants short nails because of the way they use a keyboard.

2. Which sample is short in this case?

Answer and explanation

A. The squoval sample

The brief explicitly contrasts a short squoval sample with a long almond sample.

3. What does Amara choose for discussion?

Answer and explanation

B. The short squoval appearance

The choice concerns the sample appearance, not a technique or durability guarantee.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

This comparison concerns the two supplied samples: long almond and short squoval. Squoval describes an outline, not an absolute length in every salon. The selected short appearance still needs an agreed length reference and normal assessment.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
silhouette
Outer visible outline of a nail shape.compare the silhouettes
almond
Shape narrowing toward a rounded, almond-like tip.describe the almond sample
squoval
Shape combining a straighter edge with softened corners.explain the squoval outline
square
Shape with a straighter free edge and more defined corners.identify the square shape
round
Shape with a rounded free-edge outline.compare a round shape
oval
Elongated rounded shape without a sharply pointed tip.describe an oval outline
stiletto
Shape narrowing to a pronounced pointed tip.identify the stiletto sample
coffin
Tapered shape ending in a comparatively flat tip.describe the coffin silhouette
tapered sides
Side outlines that narrow toward the tip.compare tapered sides
rounded tip
Tip with a curved rather than sharp endpoint.identify the rounded tip
softened corners
Corners with a gentler rounded outline.describe softened corners
straight edge
Outline appearing relatively flat across the end.identify the straight edge
sample display
Set of examples used to compare visible choices.use the sample display
length reference
Agreed example or measurement used to discuss length.confirm a length reference
practical requirement
Everyday need affecting the client's preference.record the practical requirement
keyboard work
Work involving regular typing or use of keys.discuss keyboard work
daily use
How the client normally uses their hands.ask about daily use
preferred outline
Shape the client selects for discussion.confirm the preferred outline
symmetry
Visual balance between corresponding sides or nails.discuss symmetry
proportion
Relationship between dimensions such as length and width.compare proportions
breakage
Breaking of a nail or enhancement, not prevented by a verbal guarantee.avoid a breakage guarantee
comfort expectation
What the client hopes will feel manageable in use.clarify the comfort expectation
measurement
Specific quantified length, not established by a broad label alone.agree on a measurement
suitability assessment
Professional review of whether the proposed service fits the actual situation.complete a suitability assessment

A client can prefer short nails for keyboard work without proving that every short shape will be comfortable or resistant to breakage. Describe appearance and practical priorities; do not convert a preference into a performance guarantee.

C Notice the language

A practical preference is not a lesser choice

Avoid praising one shape as universally better or more professional. Ask how the client uses their hands, compare the supplied samples neutrally, and preserve the stated length limit. The client should not feel pressured to copy a dramatic display sample.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask about daily use
What length feels practical for your keyboard work?
Acknowledge the attraction
You like the almond outline in this sample.
Identify the conflict
This particular almond sample is longer than your stated preference.
Describe the alternative
The squoval sample shown here is short.
Explain the corners
It has a straighter edge with softened corners.
Explain the almond tip
The almond sample narrows toward a rounded tip.
Separate shape and length
Let us compare the outline and the length as two details.
Preserve the practical limit
Your priority is a short result.
Avoid a durability promise
I cannot promise that either shape prevents breakage.
Avoid a comfort guarantee
The sample alone cannot establish how it will feel in daily use.
Check the chosen appearance
Is the short squoval sample closer to what you want?
Leave the method open
We have not agreed on a technical method yet.
Confirm a reference
We still need a clear length reference for your actual nails.
Keep the comparison neutral
The shapes look different; neither is automatically better for everyone.
Read back the priority
Short length for keyboard work, with the squoval appearance preferred.
Confirm before proceeding
We will settle the remaining details before starting.
6 language and precision notes
Shape versus length
Almond and squoval describe outlines; long and short describe relative length.
This sample
Limits the claim to the example being compared rather than every version of the shape.
Closer to
Expresses preference without promising an exact photographic copy.
Need versus guarantee
A practical need informs assessment but does not establish performance.
Rounded versus round
A rounded tip can appear within an almond shape; round is also a whole-shape label.
Before starting
Length and method questions should be resolved before an irreversible change.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which comparison is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. This almond sample is long and tapered; this squoval sample is short with softened corners.

The comparison stays with the supplied visible features and avoids universal claims.

2. Which question best clarifies the next detail?

Answer and explanation

D. What length reference shall we agree on for your actual nails?

The exact practical length remains to be clarified on the client's actual nails.

3. Which promise is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. This shape will never cause discomfort or break.

Neither comfort nor breakage prevention is established by the visual sample.

4. What does the selected sample establish?

Answer and explanation

D. A preferred appearance for discussion

The client selects the visible direction while assessment and exact details remain open.

  2. Kai
    The 1 is a short result, then. We can compare the shape you like without assuming you want the full length of this display sample.
  3. Amara
    Yes. I like the tapered shape, but that length would make typing awkward for me.
  4. Kai
    The 2 outline narrows toward a rounded tip. In this particular sample, that outline is shown at a visibly longer length.
  5. Amara
    What is the name of the shorter one beside it? The end looks straighter, but the corners do not look sharply square.
  6. Kai
    That is the 3 sample. It combines a straighter end with softened corners, and the example here is short.
  7. Amara
    I think the shorter one is closer. Could we use that shape without copying the long sample?
  8. Kai
    Certainly. We are comparing the 4 and lengths first. The technical method and the appropriate service still need their own discussion.
  9. Amara
    The shorter one seems less likely to get in my way. Does that mean it will definitely be comfortable when I type?
  10. Kai
    I cannot give a 5 from a sample. Your work needs help guide the consultation, but they do not prove how a particular result will feel on your actual nails.
  11. Amara
    Does squoval mean it won't break? A friend told me it was the strongest shape.
  12. Kai
    A 6 promise like that would be too strong. We can discuss your preferences and the assessment without claiming a shape is unbreakable.
  13. Amara
    Then let us use the short squoval as the appearance I prefer. I do not want the long almond length copied.
  14. Kai
    I will record the 7 and your short-length priority together. That keeps the visual choice connected to the practical reason you gave.
  15. Amara
    How short do you mean on my nails? Could you show me before you start?

    Yes, we still need an agreed 8 for your actual nails. The word short is useful, but it should not be the only detail in the final agreement.
  17. Amara
    Good. Please keep the softened corners in the discussion too. That is part of what I like about the second sample.
  18. Kai
    I will. 9 are part of the visible shape you prefer, separate from the exact length we still need to confirm.
  19. Amara
    Yes, short with those softer corners. Let's check the actual length together.
  20. Kai
    Correct. The 10 and remaining details still matter. We have a clear direction: short length for keyboard work, with the squoval appearance preferred.

Answers and explanations
  1. practical requirement Turn 2. Practical requirement is the client's need for short nails during keyboard work, which should remain central to the comparison.
  2. almond Turn 4. Almond identifies the sample's narrowing outline and rounded tip, separate from the long length shown in this particular example.
  3. squoval Turn 6. Squoval describes the straighter edge with softened corners shown in the shorter alternative sample.
  4. silhouettes Turn 8. Silhouettes are the visible outer outlines, not instructions about which product or technique must be used.
  5. comfort guarantee Turn 10. Comfort guarantee would promise a daily-use outcome that the sample alone cannot establish for this client.
  6. breakage Turn 12. Breakage is a possible outcome that cannot be ruled out merely by choosing a named shape or looking at a display.
  7. preferred outline Turn 14. Preferred outline is the squoval appearance Amara chooses for discussion, while rejecting the long sample's length.
  8. length reference Turn 16. Length reference is the specific example or measurement to confirm on the actual nails, beyond the broad word short.
  9. Softened corners Turn 18. Softened corners distinguish the chosen squoval appearance from a more sharply square outline in this comparison.
  10. suitability assessment Turn 20. Suitability assessment remains part of the normal professional process; selecting an appearance does not establish a method or guaranteed performance.

  2. Kai: The practical requirement is a short result, then. We can compare the shape you like without assuming you want the full length of this display sample.

  11. Amara: Does squoval mean it won't break? A friend told me it was the strongest shape.
  12. Kai: A breakage promise like that would be too strong. We can discuss your preferences and the assessment without claiming a shape is unbreakable.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A shorter sample changes the comparison

A client compares almond and squoval samples.

  1. Technician: This almond sample is longer than the squoval one. Which feature catches your attention?
  2. Client: The narrow tip looks elegant, but I type all day and want a short result.
  3. Technician: Then we need to compare length separately from the shape of the tip.
  4. Client: I thought choosing almond meant choosing exactly the length shown on the display.
  5. Technician: The display is a reference, not your final specification. Your preferred length still matters.
  6. Client: What makes the squoval sample different from the almond shape, apart from the length?
  7. Technician: Its end is flatter, with softened corners, whereas the almond outline narrows toward the tip.
  8. Client: I like the flatter end and softer corners. Let's use the shorter sample.
  9. Technician: Certainly. We'll use that reference while assessing your actual request and confirming the length.
  10. Client: That looks closer. Can you show me the length before we start?
Even length or the longest nail?

A technician checks what a client means by matching lengths.

  1. Technician: When you say make them match, do you mean similar visible length or identical decoration?
  2. Client: Similar length. One nail is much longer, and I don't want the others extended to match it.
  3. Technician: Then retaining that longest nail isn't your priority. Is a shorter overall appearance acceptable?
  4. Client: Yes, but please show me the intended length before assuming how short I mean.
  5. Technician: We can use the free edge as a visible reference during the consultation.
  6. Client: That's the part beyond my fingertip, correct? I haven't heard that term before.
  7. Technician: Correct. Point to the amount you want to keep so we can discuss it clearly.
  8. Client: About this much. I want the overall result short without confusing short with no visible edge.
  9. Technician: I understand the reference point. We'll confirm the intended outline as a separate choice.
  10. Client: Good. Similar short length is the goal, with the exact shape still to discuss.
The color swatch is on a long tip

A client worries that choosing a swatch selects its length.

  1. Technician: You chose this blue swatch. Is it the shade you like or its long pointed outline?
  2. Client: Just the shade. I was worried you would copy the plastic tip's length too.
  3. Technician: The swatch lets us compare color; its display shape doesn't determine your service request.
  4. Client: That's reassuring. I'd like the blue with the short rounded outline we discussed earlier.
  5. Technician: I'll keep those choices separate: blue shade and short rounded outline, without adding length.
  6. Client: Could I see the blue next to the gray sample before making the color final?
  7. Technician: Of course. What difference matters to you when you compare them in this light?
  8. Client: The gray looks softer. I may prefer it for the meeting I have tomorrow.
  9. Technician: Then take a moment to compare. The shape decision stays the same whichever shade you select.
  10. Client: I'll choose the gray. My earlier blue choice wasn't meant to change the length.

  2. Switch roles. Read the keyboard-work request without turning the client's preference into a guarantee against breakage.

A client compares a long oval sample with a short round sample. They choose the short round appearance for keyboard work. No exact measurement or guarantee against breakage is agreed.

Answer and explanation

Answer and explanation

Lesson 02: Comparing shape and practical length
Original fictional case: Client Amara likes a long almond sample but needs a short result for keyboard work. Technician Kai also has a short squoval sample, with a straighter free-edge outline and softened corners. The almond sample narrows toward a rounded tip and is visibly longer. Amara chooses the short squoval appearance for discussion. No method, exact measurement, or guarantee against breakage or discomfort is established. Kai must distinguish the visible comparison from a promise about how either shape will perform on the actual nails.
Language workshop: A shape that fits the working day
Communication goal: Compare sample silhouettes and lengths while preserving a practical requirement and avoiding unsupported durability claims.
Language guidance: This comparison concerns the two supplied samples: long almond and short squoval. Squoval describes an outline, not an absolute length in every salon. The selected short appearance still needs an agreed length reference and normal assessment.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What length feels practical for your keyboard work? / You like the almond outline in this sample. / This particular almond sample is longer than your stated preference. / The squoval sample shown here is short. / It has a straighter edge with softened corners. / The almond sample narrows toward a rounded tip. / Let us compare the outline and the length as two details. / Your priority is a short result. / I cannot promise that either shape prevents breakage. / The sample alone cannot establish how it will feel in daily use. / Is the short squoval sample closer to what you want? / We have not agreed on a technical method yet. / We still need a clear length reference for your actual nails. / The shapes look different; neither is automatically better for everyone. / Short length for keyboard work, with the squoval appearance preferred. / We will settle the remaining details before starting.
Vocabulary: silhouette: Outer visible outline of a nail shape. / almond: Shape narrowing toward a rounded, almond-like tip. / squoval: Shape combining a straighter edge with softened corners. / square: Shape with a straighter free edge and more defined corners. / round: Shape with a rounded free-edge outline. / oval: Elongated rounded shape without a sharply pointed tip. / stiletto: Shape narrowing to a pronounced pointed tip. / coffin: Tapered shape ending in a comparatively flat tip. / tapered sides: Side outlines that narrow toward the tip. / rounded tip: Tip with a curved rather than sharp endpoint. / softened corners: Corners with a gentler rounded outline. / straight edge: Outline appearing relatively flat across the end. / sample display: Set of examples used to compare visible choices. / length reference: Agreed example or measurement used to discuss length. / practical requirement: Everyday need affecting the client's preference. / keyboard work: Work involving regular typing or use of keys. / daily use: How the client normally uses their hands. / preferred outline: Shape the client selects for discussion. / symmetry: Visual balance between corresponding sides or nails. / proportion: Relationship between dimensions such as length and width. / breakage: Breaking of a nail or enhancement, not prevented by a verbal guarantee. / comfort expectation: What the client hopes will feel manageable in use. / measurement: Specific quantified length, not established by a broad label alone. / suitability assessment: Professional review of whether the proposed service fits the actual situation.
Speaking task: Read the comparison using almond, squoval, rounded tip, and softened corners. Switch roles. Read the keyboard-work request without turning the client's preference into a guarantee against breakage. Complete and check the round-shape transfer, then read the shape and length as separate choices.
Partner: Amara: Client / Kai: Nail technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A client compares a long oval sample with a short round sample. They choose the short round appearance for keyboard work. No exact measurement or guarantee against breakage is agreed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Amara: Good. Please keep the softened corners in the discussion too. That is part of what I like about the second sample.
Kai: I will. Softened corners are part of the visible shape you prefer, separate from the exact length we still need to confirm.
Amara: Yes, short with those softer corners. Let's check the actual length together.
Kai: Correct. The suitability assessment and remaining details still matter. We have a clear direction: short length for keyboard work, with the squoval appearance preferred.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.

Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.

Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

03Explaining color, coverage, and finishSheer pink, without the shine+

Your goal

Separate color, coverage, and finish while leaving an unspecified product system open for consultation.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Sheer pink, without the shine

Client Dana asks technician Noor for matte pink but points to a sheer glossy swatch. Dana explains that the desired result has low shine while the natural nail line remains visible. The selected swatch illustrates the preferred pink color and transparency, but not the preferred finish. The booking does not specify conventional lacquer or gel polish. Noor records the appearance as sheer pink with a low-shine finish, leaving product selection and suitability open rather than assuming the swatch chooses the whole service.

Dana: Client / Noor: Nail technician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which appearance does Dana want?

Answer and explanation

A. Sheer pink with low shine

Dana wants the pink and transparency of the swatch but a lower-shine finish.

2. What should remain visible?

Answer and explanation

D. The natural nail line

The client specifically wants the nail line visible through the color.

3. Which product category is booked?

Answer and explanation

D. Neither lacquer nor gel polish is specified

The booking leaves the product category unspecified, so it requires clarification.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Matte and sheer answer different questions. Matte concerns shine; sheer concerns how much underlying nail remains visible. The client can request both. The glossy swatch illustrates the preferred color and coverage, not the agreed final shine.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
swatch
Sample showing a color or finish for comparison.compare the swatch
shade
Particular variation of a color.choose a pink shade
hue
Basic color family, such as pink or red.describe the hue
undertone
Subtle underlying color character.compare undertones
coverage
Degree to which the applied color conceals what is beneath it.clarify the coverage
sheer
Translucent enough for some underlying nail detail to remain visible.request sheer coverage
semi-sheer
Partially translucent, between very sheer and fully opaque.compare semi-sheer coverage
opaque
Concealing the underlying nail rather than allowing it to show through.distinguish opaque coverage
translucency
Degree to which light and underlying detail show through.describe translucency
nail line
Visible boundary toward the nail's free edge, used here as an appearance reference.retain a visible nail line
finish
Surface appearance, including the level of shine.clarify the finish
matte
Low-shine or non-glossy surface appearance.request a matte finish
glossy
Shiny surface appearance.identify the glossy swatch
satin
Soft-sheen appearance between matte and high gloss in a given range.compare a satin finish
sheen
Degree of reflected shine.describe the sheen
shimmer
Fine reflective sparkle within a color effect.identify shimmer
glitter
Visible reflective particles used for a decorative effect.distinguish glitter from gloss
metallic
Appearance resembling reflective metal.describe a metallic effect
pearlescent
Soft luminous effect resembling the sheen of a pearl.compare a pearlescent effect
top coat
Finishing coating whose properties depend on the product system.discuss the top coat
color coat
Layer providing the selected color effect.identify the color coat
product system
Compatible set of products and procedures for a service.confirm the product system
appearance specification
Separate agreed details of color, coverage, and finish.record the appearance specification
curing lamp
Light source specified for curing a compatible gel system; not selected by wattage alone.verify the curing lamp

A swatch is not a product-selection shortcut. The booking does not identify lacquer or gel polish, and the appearance request does not determine compatibility, suitability, or application steps. Those details remain within the actual consultation.

C Notice the language

Separate the details kindly

A client is not being inconsistent when one sample illustrates only part of a request. Ask which features they like and translate the answer into separate appearance choices. Avoid overwhelming the client with product names before the desired color, coverage, and finish are clear.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Separate the choices
Let us check color, coverage, and finish one at a time.
Confirm the color
Pink is the color you prefer.
Ask about visibility
Do you want the natural nail line to remain visible?
Explain sheer
Sheer coverage allows some nail detail to show through.
Explain opaque
Opaque coverage conceals the underlying nail.
Explain matte
Matte describes a low-shine finish.
Describe the swatch
This swatch is sheer and glossy.
Identify the mismatch
You like its pink color and coverage, but not its shine.
Confirm the combined request
You want sheer pink with a low-shine finish.
Avoid a false conflict
Sheer and matte describe different features, so they are not opposites.
Clarify the booking
The booking does not specify lacquer or gel polish.
Keep product choice open
We still need to discuss the product system.
Avoid copying everything
Selecting this swatch does not select every feature it shows.
Ask for a read-back
Is visible nail line and low shine the right summary?
Avoid an application promise
We have not confirmed the product or method yet.
Close the appearance discussion
The appearance is clear; product selection remains open.
6 language and precision notes
Coverage versus finish
Coverage concerns concealment; finish concerns surface appearance.
Sheer versus clear
Sheer color can be tinted while still allowing underlying detail to show.
Matte versus opaque
Low shine does not necessarily mean full concealment of the nail.
But not
This phrase selects some sample features while explicitly rejecting another.
Unspecified
An absent product category is a question to clarify, not permission to guess.
One at a time
Separating dimensions reduces confusion without turning the conversation into a lecture.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement explains the client's request?

Answer and explanation

B. Sheer concerns visibility through the color; matte concerns shine.

Coverage and finish are independent appearance dimensions in this consultation.

2. Which swatch feature is not wanted?

Answer and explanation

C. Its glossy finish

The client likes the color and transparency but asks for low shine.

3. Which record is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Sheer pink, low shine, product category not yet specified.

The record preserves the three appearance details and unresolved product choice.

4. Which question best checks coverage?

Answer and explanation

A. Do you want the nail line to show through?

Visibility of the nail line directly clarifies the desired coverage.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Sheer pink, without the shine

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dana: Client / Noor: Nail technician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • sheer
  • product system
  • appearance specification
  • finish
  • sample mismatch
  • coverage
  • matte
  • Opaque
  • product category
  • nail line
  1. Dana
    I want matte pink, like this sample. I like seeing a little of the nail through it, but I don't want it so shiny.
  2. Noor
    Let's check 1 first. Do you want the natural nail line to show through the pink?
  3. Dana
    Yes. I want some color, just not a solid block that hides the nail underneath.
  4. Noor
    That's 2 coverage. It describes the see-through quality, not the shine on top.
  5. Dana
    Oh, I thought sheer meant shiny. The sheer samples I looked at all seemed glossy.
  6. Noor
    Those samples combine two features. Their 3 is glossy, but their coverage is sheer.
  7. Dana
    Then this pink is right, and the transparency is right. It's the shiny surface I'd like changed.
  8. Noor
    I'll record 4 as the finish preference. That keeps the low-shine request separate from the color.
  9. Dana
    What would opaque look like? Is that another word for dark pink, or does it mean something else?
  10. Noor
    5 means you wouldn't see the underlying nail through the color. Pale colors can be opaque too.
  11. Dana
    Then I don't mean opaque. Please keep the nail line visible even though I don't want a glossy finish.
  12. Noor
    Yes. The 6 stays in the appearance description. Low shine doesn't mean it has to be hidden.
  13. Dana
    Have I already booked gel polish? I don't remember choosing between that and ordinary polish on the form.
  14. Noor
    No 7 is listed. We should discuss lacquer and gel polish separately from this swatch.
  15. Dana
    Could you explain the difference before I choose? I don't want to agree just because I pointed to this one.
  16. Noor
    Of course. We'll discuss the 8 and the service involved. The sample has only helped clarify the look.
  17. Dana
    So my request is pink, slightly see-through, and not shiny. Is that the right way to put it?
  18. Noor
    Yes. That's a clear 9: sheer pink with low shine.
  19. Dana
    Great. Keep those three points together, please. I'd be disappointed if matte turned into a completely solid pink.
  20. Noor
    I'll note the 10 too: you like this swatch's color and transparency, but not its glossy surface.

Answers and explanations
  1. coverage Turn 2. Coverage concerns how much underlying nail detail is concealed, not the amount of reflected shine.
  2. sheer Turn 4. Sheer coverage allows some underlying detail to show through a tinted color.
  3. finish Turn 6. Finish describes the surface appearance, which can be discussed separately from coverage.
  4. matte Turn 8. Matte identifies Dana's requested low-shine surface, unlike the glossy reference.
  5. Opaque Turn 10. Opaque means the underlying nail is concealed; it does not simply mean the color is dark.
  6. nail line Turn 12. The nail line is the underlying detail Dana wants to retain through the sheer pink coverage.
  7. product category Turn 14. The booking specifies neither lacquer nor gel polish, so the appearance request cannot settle that choice.
  8. product system Turn 16. The product system is still to be selected through consultation, not inferred from the chosen color sample.
  9. appearance specification Turn 18. The appearance specification records color, coverage, and finish together without selecting application products.
  10. sample mismatch Turn 20. The mismatch is limited to the sample's gloss, not its pink color or sheer coverage.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dana: I want matte pink, like this sample. I like seeing a little of the nail through it, but I don't want it so shiny.
  2. Noor: Let's check coverage first. Do you want the natural nail line to show through the pink?
  3. Dana: Yes. I want some color, just not a solid block that hides the nail underneath.
  4. Noor: That's sheer coverage. It describes the see-through quality, not the shine on top.
  5. Dana: Oh, I thought sheer meant shiny. The sheer samples I looked at all seemed glossy.
  6. Noor: Those samples combine two features. Their finish is glossy, but their coverage is sheer.
  7. Dana: Then this pink is right, and the transparency is right. It's the shiny surface I'd like changed.
  8. Noor: I'll record matte as the finish preference. That keeps the low-shine request separate from the color.
  9. Dana: What would opaque look like? Is that another word for dark pink, or does it mean something else?
  10. Noor: Opaque means you wouldn't see the underlying nail through the color. Pale colors can be opaque too.
  11. Dana: Then I don't mean opaque. Please keep the nail line visible even though I don't want a glossy finish.
  12. Noor: Yes. The nail line stays in the appearance description. Low shine doesn't mean it has to be hidden.
  13. Dana: Have I already booked gel polish? I don't remember choosing between that and ordinary polish on the form.
  14. Noor: No product category is listed. We should discuss lacquer and gel polish separately from this swatch.
  15. Dana: Could you explain the difference before I choose? I don't want to agree just because I pointed to this one.
  16. Noor: Of course. We'll discuss the product system and the service involved. The sample has only helped clarify the look.
  17. Dana: So my request is pink, slightly see-through, and not shiny. Is that the right way to put it?
  18. Noor: Yes. That's a clear appearance specification: sheer pink with low shine.
  19. Dana: Great. Keep those three points together, please. I'd be disappointed if matte turned into a completely solid pink.
  20. Noor: I'll note the sample mismatch too: you like this swatch's color and transparency, but not its glossy surface.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Sheer and matte answer different questions

A technician clarifies a pink color request.

  1. Technician: You asked for matte pink, but chose a glossy sheer sample. What matters most?
  2. Client: I want the nail line to show, with less shine than this display tip.
  3. Technician: Then sheer describes the coverage you like, and matte describes the low-shine finish.
  4. Client: I thought sheer meant not shiny. Thanks for separating those words for me.
  5. Technician: The sample shows your preferred coverage, but doesn't display both of your requested features.
  6. Client: Does choosing that pink also mean I'm choosing gel polish for today's appointment?
  7. Technician: Not automatically. Color, finish, and product choice are separate parts of our consultation.
  8. Client: Then please leave the product choice open while we compare the available descriptions.
  9. Technician: Certainly. Your current request is sheer pink with low shine, not added opacity.
  10. Client: That's accurate. I'd like to see an appropriate sample before agreeing to the final choice.
A color name does not identify a product

A client brings an old receipt showing only a shade name.

  1. Technician: The receipt says Rose Garden, but doesn't identify whether the previous service used gel polish.
  2. Client: I assumed the color name would tell you. It looked like an ordinary pink.
  3. Technician: The same shade name can appear on different products, so I shouldn't infer the system.
  4. Client: Could you look at the old appointment record instead of guessing from the photograph?
  5. Technician: Yes. We can check whether the product was recorded and keep any missing detail explicit.
  6. Client: Would the fact that it stayed shiny tell you which product it definitely was?
  7. Technician: No. Appearance alone doesn't establish the exact product used in that previous service.
  8. Client: I can ask the original salon for details if our record doesn't show them.
  9. Technician: That would help. For now I'll describe the product as unidentified, not as confirmed lacquer.
  10. Client: Please do. I'd rather have an accurate question than a confident answer based on the name.
Opaque was not the requested effect

A client compares two pink samples at reception.

  1. Technician: Both samples are pink, but this one hides the nail line more completely.
  2. Client: Then I prefer the other one. I don't want the whole nail covered by solid-looking color.
  3. Technician: You're describing a sheer effect rather than the opaque appearance of the first sample.
  4. Client: Exactly. The word pink alone didn't explain what I had in mind originally.
  5. Technician: Would you like to compare them against a neutral background before deciding today?
  6. Client: Yes, because the dark display stand makes the lighter one difficult for me to judge.
  7. Technician: Here they are together. We can discuss the difference without assuming a precise result on your nails.
  8. Client: I'll choose this more transparent-looking pink. Please keep that sample beside the other finish options.
  9. Technician: I'll record that coverage preference and keep the finish choice separate for our next question.
  10. Client: Good. I still want to discuss shine, but the coverage question is now settled.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Sheer pink, without the shine
  1. Read the swatch dialogue. Stress sheer for coverage and matte for shine.
  2. Switch roles and read the opaque explanation; preserve the distinction between pale color and see-through coverage.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Decode another color request

A client wants opaque blue with a glossy finish. They do not want the nail line visible. The booking leaves lacquer versus gel polish unspecified.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Technician: "The requested color is ___."

Answer and explanation

A. blue

Blue is the chosen color family in this new scenario.

2. Client: "I want ___ coverage."

Answer and explanation

D. opaque

Opaque matches the wish to conceal the underlying nail line.

3. Technician: "The requested finish is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. glossy

Glossy specifies the chosen shine independently of coverage.

4. Reception: "The product category remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unspecified

The appearance request does not choose lacquer or gel polish.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.
