Lesson 03: Explaining color, coverage, and finish
Original fictional case: Client Dana asks technician Noor for matte pink but points to a sheer glossy swatch. Dana explains that the desired result has low shine while the natural nail line remains visible. The selected swatch illustrates the preferred pink color and transparency, but not the preferred finish. The booking does not specify conventional lacquer or gel polish. Noor records the appearance as sheer pink with a low-shine finish, leaving product selection and suitability open rather than assuming the swatch chooses the whole service.
Language workshop: Sheer pink, without the shine
Communication goal: Separate color, coverage, and finish while leaving an unspecified product system open for consultation.
Language guidance: Matte and sheer answer different questions. Matte concerns shine; sheer concerns how much underlying nail remains visible. The client can request both. The glossy swatch illustrates the preferred color and coverage, not the agreed final shine.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Let us check color, coverage, and finish one at a time. / Pink is the color you prefer. / Do you want the natural nail line to remain visible? / Sheer coverage allows some nail detail to show through. / Opaque coverage conceals the underlying nail. / Matte describes a low-shine finish. / This swatch is sheer and glossy. / You like its pink color and coverage, but not its shine. / You want sheer pink with a low-shine finish. / Sheer and matte describe different features, so they are not opposites. / The booking does not specify lacquer or gel polish. / We still need to discuss the product system. / Selecting this swatch does not select every feature it shows. / Is visible nail line and low shine the right summary? / We have not confirmed the product or method yet. / The appearance is clear; product selection remains open.
Vocabulary: swatch: Sample showing a color or finish for comparison. / shade: Particular variation of a color. / hue: Basic color family, such as pink or red. / undertone: Subtle underlying color character. / coverage: Degree to which the applied color conceals what is beneath it. / sheer: Translucent enough for some underlying nail detail to remain visible. / semi-sheer: Partially translucent, between very sheer and fully opaque. / opaque: Concealing the underlying nail rather than allowing it to show through. / translucency: Degree to which light and underlying detail show through. / nail line: Visible boundary toward the nail's free edge, used here as an appearance reference. / finish: Surface appearance, including the level of shine. / matte: Low-shine or non-glossy surface appearance. / glossy: Shiny surface appearance. / satin: Soft-sheen appearance between matte and high gloss in a given range. / sheen: Degree of reflected shine. / shimmer: Fine reflective sparkle within a color effect. / glitter: Visible reflective particles used for a decorative effect. / metallic: Appearance resembling reflective metal. / pearlescent: Soft luminous effect resembling the sheen of a pearl. / top coat: Finishing coating whose properties depend on the product system. / color coat: Layer providing the selected color effect. / product system: Compatible set of products and procedures for a service. / appearance specification: Separate agreed details of color, coverage, and finish. / curing lamp: Light source specified for curing a compatible gel system; not selected by wattage alone.
Speaking task: Read the swatch dialogue. Stress sheer for coverage and matte for shine. Switch roles and read the opaque explanation; preserve the distinction between pale color and see-through coverage. Complete and check the blue-color transfer. Read color, coverage, finish, and the unresolved product category separately.
Partner: Dana: Client / Noor: Nail technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A client wants opaque blue with a glossy finish. They do not want the nail line visible. The booking leaves lacquer versus gel polish unspecified.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dana: So my request is pink, slightly see-through, and not shiny. Is that the right way to put it?
Noor: Yes. That's a clear appearance specification: sheer pink with low shine.
Dana: Great. Keep those three points together, please. I'd be disappointed if matte turned into a completely solid pink.
Noor: I'll note the sample mismatch too: you like this swatch's color and transparency, but not its glossy surface.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Agreeing on nail art and a complete quoteTwo accent nails within forty dollars+

Your goal

Calculate a per-nail addition, offer complete priced alternatives, and confirm quantity and placement before starting.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

Two accent nails within forty dollars

Client Ben wants simple line art on four nails but has a total limit of $40. Technician Rosa explains this fictional menu: the base service is $30, simple line art is $5 per nail, and no other charges apply in the exercise. Four decorated nails would cost $50. Two decorated nails would cost $40, or the base service alone would cost $30. Ben chooses two decorated nails, one ring fingernail on each hand. Rosa confirms that two means the total across both hands, not two on each hand.

Ben: Client / Rosa: Nail technician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What would four decorated nails cost in total?

Answer and explanation

B. $50

Four nails at five dollars add twenty dollars to the thirty-dollar base.

2. Which option meets the $40 limit with art?

Answer and explanation

A. Two decorated nails plus the base service

Two nails add ten dollars, making the complete total forty dollars.

3. Which placement is selected?

Answer and explanation

A. One ring fingernail on each hand

One ring fingernail on each hand gives the agreed total of two decorated nails.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Four decorated nails cost $20 in art charges, making $50 with the base service. Two decorated nails cost $10 in art charges, making $40 total. Two on each hand would be four in total and exceed the stated budget.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
nail art
Decorative design added to the nail appearance.specify the nail art
line art
Design formed primarily with lines.quote simple line art
accent nail
Nail given a distinct decorative treatment from the others.select an accent nail
decorated nail
Nail included in the agreed art quantity.count decorated nails
plain color
Color without the additional decorative design.choose plain color
placement
Specific nail or area where the design appears.confirm design placement
orientation
Direction in which a design faces.clarify the orientation
motif
Repeated or distinct decorative element.choose a motif
stripe
Long narrow decorative line or band.describe a stripe
dot detail
Decorative element made of small dots.clarify the dot detail
negative space
Deliberately uncovered area within a design.identify negative space
French tip
Design emphasizing a contrasting appearance at the nail tip.discuss a French-tip design
ombre
Gradual visual transition between colors or tones.describe an ombre effect
per-nail charge
Price applying to each decorated nail individually.explain the per-nail charge
base price
Cost of the underlying service before art additions.state the base price
art subtotal
Total charge for the decorative additions alone.calculate the art subtotal
complete quote
Total price for the specifically described option.give a complete quote
budget limit
Maximum total the client agrees to spend.respect the budget limit
smile line
Curved boundary between a contrasting French tip and the rest of the nail design.describe a crisp smile line
ring fingernail
Nail on the finger between the middle and little fingers.identify the ring fingernail
each hand
Separately referring to the left and right hands.confirm one on each hand
both hands
Referring to the two hands together.total across both hands
design approval
Agreement to the specified design and placement.obtain design approval
additional charge
Amount added beyond the base service.state the additional charge

The accepted option is two decorated nails in total, specifically one ring fingernail on each hand. Do not quietly reduce the original four-nail request or increase the selected two-nail version. Quantity and placement both need agreement.

C Notice the language

Make the quantity visible in the price

Per-nail charges can be misunderstood as per-hand or whole-set prices. State the quantity, the additional amount, and the complete total together. Offering a smaller design within a budget is helpful only if the client explicitly chooses the change.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask about the limit
Is $40 your total budget, including the base service?
State the base price
The base service is $30.
Explain the unit charge
Simple line art is $5 per decorated nail.
Calculate the first option
Four decorated nails add $20, making $50 total.
Offer the smaller option
Two decorated nails add $10, making $40 total.
Preserve the plain option
The base service alone is $30.
Keep totals complete
These totals include the base service and the stated art quantity.
Ask for a choice
Would you prefer two decorated nails or the base service alone?
Confirm the reduction
You are choosing two instead of the original four.
Clarify both hands
Do you mean two in total across both hands?
Check placement
Which two nails would you like decorated?
Name the fingers
One ring fingernail on each hand gives two in total.
Avoid silent additions
We will confirm any additional art before changing the price.
Read back the design
Simple line art on the two ring fingernails.
Read back the total
The agreed total is $40.
Confirm before starting
Let us settle quantity, placement, and price before beginning.
6 language and precision notes
Per versus total
Per nail gives a unit charge; total includes all selected nails and the base service.
Each versus both
One on each hand equals two across both hands.
Subtotal
The art subtotal excludes the base price and is not the amount for the entire service.
Instead of
This marks an accepted change from four decorated nails to two.
Up to
A budget up to $40 is a limit, not permission to exceed it with unmentioned additions.
Placement
Agreeing on two nails still leaves the specific fingers to be confirmed.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which quote is complete?

Answer and explanation

A. Two decorated nails at $10 plus the $30 base: $40 total.

The correct quote states quantity, art subtotal, base price, and final total.

2. What does one ring fingernail on each hand mean?

Answer and explanation

B. Two decorated nails in total

One selected finger on each of two hands gives two decorated nails.

3. Which response wrongly assumes agreement?

Answer and explanation

C. I reduced your four-nail design without asking so the bill fits.

A budget constraint does not authorize silently changing the requested design quantity.

4. Which change would exceed the accepted quantity?

Answer and explanation

B. Decorating two nails on each hand

Two per hand produces four decorated nails rather than the accepted two.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

Two accent nails within forty dollars

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Client / Rosa: Nail technician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • design approval
  • base price
  • placement
  • art subtotal
  • both hands
  • accent nail
  • budget limit
  • complete quote
  • per-nail charge
  • quantity reduction
  1. Ben
    I would like the simple line design on four nails, but I need to keep the whole appointment at forty dollars. Does that work?
  2. Rosa
    Let me give you the 1 before we choose. The base service is thirty dollars, and simple line art is five dollars for each decorated nail.
  3. Ben
    I had thought five dollars meant the whole design, not five for every nail. What would the four-nail version come to?
  4. Rosa
    The 2 makes four decorated nails twenty dollars extra. Added to the thirty-dollar base service, that gives a total of fifty dollars.
  5. Ben
    That is more than I can spend today. Is there a smaller version that stays within forty without changing the base service?
  6. Rosa
    Two decorated nails would have an 3 of ten dollars. With the base service, the complete total would be forty dollars.
  7. Ben
    And if I decide not to have any art at all, would the base service still be available at thirty?
  8. Rosa
    Yes. The 4 is thirty dollars without the art. Your two choices within the limit are the base alone or the base with two decorated nails.
  9. Ben
    Let's do two with the line design, then. I can manage forty, but not fifty today.
  10. Rosa
    I will confirm that 5 with you: two decorated nails instead of four, for forty dollars in total.
  11. Ben
    When you say two, is that two on each hand or two altogether? I want to make sure I am counting the same way.
  12. Rosa
    Two altogether, across 6. Two on each hand would be four nails and would return the total to fifty dollars.
  13. Ben
    Then one on each hand sounds right. I would like the ring fingernails decorated, with the other nails left without that line design.
  14. Rosa
    That gives one 7 on each hand. I will specify the two ring fingernails so the placement is not left to an assumption.
  15. Ben
    Yes, these two. Please leave the others plain; I like the design better on the ring fingers.
  16. Rosa
    The 8 is now clear: the ring fingernail on your left hand and the ring fingernail on your right.
  17. Ben
    Good. And forty is still the complete total, not forty plus another amount for selecting those fingers?
  18. Rosa
    Correct. Your 9 is met by this option. There are no other charges in this comparison, and the two-nail art total remains ten dollars.
  19. Ben
    That's agreed: both ring fingernails, forty dollars altogether. No extra art today.
  20. Rosa
    I have your 10 for that exact option. We will confirm any later change before altering the quantity, placement, or price, rather than adding something without asking.

Answers and explanations
  1. complete quote Turn 2. Complete quote includes the base service and all selected art, so the client can compare the actual total with the budget.
  2. per-nail charge Turn 4. Per-nail charge applies five dollars to each of four nails, creating twenty dollars in art charges rather than one five-dollar fee.
  3. art subtotal Turn 6. Art subtotal is ten dollars for two nails and must be added to the thirty-dollar base to reach the complete forty-dollar price.
  4. base price Turn 8. Base price remains thirty dollars for the service without the optional line art, giving a second option within the client's limit.
  5. quantity reduction Turn 10. Quantity reduction is the client's explicit choice to move from four decorated nails to two, not a silent change made by the technician.
  6. both hands Turn 12. Both hands means the combined total for left and right, so two across both hands is not two on each hand.
  7. accent nail Turn 14. Accent nail is the individually decorated nail, here one ring fingernail on each hand for two in total.
  8. placement Turn 16. Placement identifies the exact nails receiving the art, which is a separate detail from the overall quantity of two.
  9. budget limit Turn 18. Budget limit is forty dollars for the entire selected service, and the confirmed option meets it with no other charges in this fictional menu.
  10. design approval Turn 20. Design approval covers the specified two ring fingernails, simple line art, and forty-dollar total, not further unrequested additions.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: I would like the simple line design on four nails, but I need to keep the whole appointment at forty dollars. Does that work?
  2. Rosa: Let me give you the complete quote before we choose. The base service is thirty dollars, and simple line art is five dollars for each decorated nail.
  3. Ben: I had thought five dollars meant the whole design, not five for every nail. What would the four-nail version come to?
  4. Rosa: The per-nail charge makes four decorated nails twenty dollars extra. Added to the thirty-dollar base service, that gives a total of fifty dollars.
  5. Ben: That is more than I can spend today. Is there a smaller version that stays within forty without changing the base service?
  6. Rosa: Two decorated nails would have an art subtotal of ten dollars. With the base service, the complete total would be forty dollars.
  7. Ben: And if I decide not to have any art at all, would the base service still be available at thirty?
  8. Rosa: Yes. The base price is thirty dollars without the art. Your two choices within the limit are the base alone or the base with two decorated nails.
  9. Ben: Let's do two with the line design, then. I can manage forty, but not fifty today.
  10. Rosa: I will confirm that quantity reduction with you: two decorated nails instead of four, for forty dollars in total.
  11. Ben: When you say two, is that two on each hand or two altogether? I want to make sure I am counting the same way.
  12. Rosa: Two altogether, across both hands. Two on each hand would be four nails and would return the total to fifty dollars.
  13. Ben: Then one on each hand sounds right. I would like the ring fingernails decorated, with the other nails left without that line design.
  14. Rosa: That gives one accent nail on each hand. I will specify the two ring fingernails so the placement is not left to an assumption.
  15. Ben: Yes, these two. Please leave the others plain; I like the design better on the ring fingers.
  16. Rosa: The placement is now clear: the ring fingernail on your left hand and the ring fingernail on your right.
  17. Ben: Good. And forty is still the complete total, not forty plus another amount for selecting those fingers?
  18. Rosa: Correct. Your budget limit is met by this option. There are no other charges in this comparison, and the two-nail art total remains ten dollars.
  19. Ben: That's agreed: both ring fingernails, forty dollars altogether. No extra art today.
  20. Rosa: I have your design approval for that exact option. We will confirm any later change before altering the quantity, placement, or price, rather than adding something without asking.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Four designs exceed the stated limit

A client has a forty-dollar budget. Fictional final prices are thirty for the base service and five per line-art nail, with no other charges.

  1. Technician: The base service is thirty dollars, and this line art costs five per nail.
  2. Client: I wanted four decorated nails, but my budget for the whole appointment is forty.
  3. Technician: Four would make fifty. Two accent nails would bring the complete total to forty.
  4. Client: Could we put the design on one nail on each hand instead of four?
  5. Technician: Yes. Which two nails do you want marked in the agreed design request?
  6. Client: The ring finger on each hand, please. All the others can have the plain color.
  7. Technician: The base and those two line-art nails come to forty dollars altogether for today's service.
  8. Client: Does that include the little stones in the photograph, or just the painted lines?
  9. Technician: Just the lines. The stones aren't included in this quote or your current design.
  10. Client: Leave the stones out, then. Two line-art accent nails and a forty-dollar total work.
The design has a front and a back

A technician checks the orientation of lettering in a design.

  1. Technician: For the lettering, which direction should it face when you look down at your hands?
  2. Client: I hadn't thought about that. I want to read it myself while my hands rest flat.
  3. Technician: That differs from lettering oriented toward someone looking at your fingertips from across a table.
  4. Client: Then please use the direction for me, not the direction in the social-media picture.
  5. Technician: Let's check this sample orientation before including it in the design agreement today.
  6. Client: This sample is right. Could both hands follow the same reading direction rather than mirror each other?
  7. Technician: We can record that request clearly, then confirm the final design and price together.
  8. Client: One letter on each ring-finger nail, one per hand, not the whole word.
  9. Technician: Thank you. I'll count two lettered nails and keep the other nails plain.
  10. Client: That description matches my intention. Please quote those two letters rather than the photographed full word.
An embellishment was only an idea

A client clarifies that a design question was not a purchase decision.

  1. Technician: You asked about adding an embellishment. Would you like a price before deciding whether to include it?
  2. Client: Yes. I was asking how it would look, not agreeing to pay for it yet.
  3. Technician: Understood. Our current quote is for plain color only, with no decorative additions.
  4. Client: Could you explain whether the displayed extra price is for one nail or the full set?
  5. Technician: This label is per decorated nail. The number chosen changes the total for the appointment.
  6. Client: Then I need a complete quote before choosing. I may decide the plain version is enough.
  7. Technician: Certainly. For two embellished nails, the extra would be twelve dollars above your existing quote.
  8. Client: That's more than I planned. I'll keep the plain color and skip the embellishments today.
  9. Technician: I'll retain the original quote and remove the proposed additions from our discussion summary.
  10. Client: Thank you. Looking at options shouldn't leave the impression that I've already ordered them.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Two accent nails within forty dollars
  1. Read the quotation aloud: $30 base, $5 per nail, four nails $50 total, two nails $40 total.
  2. Switch roles for the placement exchange. Say one on each hand and two altogether distinctly.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Recalculate another art quote

A base service costs $35. Art costs $4 per nail, with no other charges. The client has a $43 limit and chooses one decorated nail on each hand.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Technician: "The selected quantity is ___ nails."

Answer and explanation

D. two

One decorated nail on each of two hands gives two nails total.

2. Client: "The art subtotal is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

C. eight

Two nails multiplied by four dollars gives eight dollars for art.

3. Technician: "The complete total is ___ dollars."

Answer and explanation

C. forty-three

The thirty-five-dollar base plus eight dollars of art totals forty-three.

4. Client: "That total meets my budget ___."

Answer and explanation

D. limit

The total equals the stated maximum without exceeding it.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 04: Agreeing on nail art and a complete quote
Original fictional case: Client Ben wants simple line art on four nails but has a total limit of $40. Technician Rosa explains this fictional menu: the base service is $30, simple line art is $5 per nail, and no other charges apply in the exercise. Four decorated nails would cost $50. Two decorated nails would cost $40, or the base service alone would cost $30. Ben chooses two decorated nails, one ring fingernail on each hand. Rosa confirms that two means the total across both hands, not two on each hand.
Language workshop: Two accent nails within forty dollars
Communication goal: Calculate a per-nail addition, offer complete priced alternatives, and confirm quantity and placement before starting.
Language guidance: Four decorated nails cost $20 in art charges, making $50 with the base service. Two decorated nails cost $10 in art charges, making $40 total. Two on each hand would be four in total and exceed the stated budget.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Is $40 your total budget, including the base service? / The base service is $30. / Simple line art is $5 per decorated nail. / Four decorated nails add $20, making $50 total. / Two decorated nails add $10, making $40 total. / The base service alone is $30. / These totals include the base service and the stated art quantity. / Would you prefer two decorated nails or the base service alone? / You are choosing two instead of the original four. / Do you mean two in total across both hands? / Which two nails would you like decorated? / One ring fingernail on each hand gives two in total. / We will confirm any additional art before changing the price. / Simple line art on the two ring fingernails. / The agreed total is $40. / Let us settle quantity, placement, and price before beginning.
Vocabulary: nail art: Decorative design added to the nail appearance. / line art: Design formed primarily with lines. / accent nail: Nail given a distinct decorative treatment from the others. / decorated nail: Nail included in the agreed art quantity. / plain color: Color without the additional decorative design. / placement: Specific nail or area where the design appears. / orientation: Direction in which a design faces. / motif: Repeated or distinct decorative element. / stripe: Long narrow decorative line or band. / dot detail: Decorative element made of small dots. / negative space: Deliberately uncovered area within a design. / French tip: Design emphasizing a contrasting appearance at the nail tip. / ombre: Gradual visual transition between colors or tones. / per-nail charge: Price applying to each decorated nail individually. / base price: Cost of the underlying service before art additions. / art subtotal: Total charge for the decorative additions alone. / complete quote: Total price for the specifically described option. / budget limit: Maximum total the client agrees to spend. / smile line: Curved boundary between a contrasting French tip and the rest of the nail design. / ring fingernail: Nail on the finger between the middle and little fingers. / each hand: Separately referring to the left and right hands. / both hands: Referring to the two hands together. / design approval: Agreement to the specified design and placement. / additional charge: Amount added beyond the base service.
Speaking task: Read the quotation aloud: $30 base, $5 per nail, four nails $50 total, two nails $40 total. Switch roles for the placement exchange. Say one on each hand and two altogether distinctly. Complete the new $35-plus-art transfer, check the calculation, and read the complete $43 total rather than only the art subtotal.
Partner: Ben: Client / Rosa: Nail technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A base service costs $35. Art costs $4 per nail, with no other charges. The client has a $43 limit and chooses one decorated nail on each hand.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: Good. And forty is still the complete total, not forty plus another amount for selecting those fingers?
Rosa: Correct. Your budget limit is met by this option. There are no other charges in this comparison, and the two-nail art total remains ten dollars.
Ben: That's agreed: both ring fingernails, forty dollars altogether. No extra art today.
Rosa: I have your design approval for that exact option. We will confirm any later change before altering the quantity, placement, or price, rather than adding something without asking.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Vocabulary: nail art: Decorative design added to the nail appearance. / line art: Design formed primarily with lines. / accent nail: Nail given a distinct decorative treatment from the others. / decorated nail: Nail included in the agreed art quantity. / plain color: Color without the additional decorative design. / placement: Specific nail or area where the design appears. / orientation: Direction in which a design faces. / motif: Repeated or distinct decorative element. / stripe: Long narrow decorative line or band. / dot detail: Decorative element made of small dots. / negative space: Deliberately uncovered area within a design. / French tip: Design emphasizing a contrasting appearance at the nail tip. / ombre: Gradual visual transition between colors or tones. / per-nail charge: Price applying to each decorated nail individually. / base price: Cost of the underlying service before art additions. / art subtotal: Total charge for the decorative additions alone. / complete quote: Total price for the specifically described option. / budget limit: Maximum total the client agrees to spend. / smile line: Curved boundary between a contrasting French tip and the rest of the nail design. / ring fingernail: Nail on the finger between the middle and little fingers. / each hand: Separately referring to the left and right hands. / both hands: Referring to the two hands together. / design approval: Agreement to the specified design and placement. / additional charge: Amount added beyond the base service.
Speaking task: Read the quotation aloud: $30 base, $5 per nail, four nails $50 total, two nails $40 total. Switch roles for the placement exchange. Say one on each hand and two altogether distinctly. Complete the new $35-plus-art transfer, check the calculation, and read the complete $43 total rather than only the art subtotal.
Partner: Ben: Client / Rosa: Nail technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A base service costs $35. Art costs $4 per nail, with no other charges. The client has a $43 limit and chooses one decorated nail on each hand.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: Good. And forty is still the complete total, not forty plus another amount for selecting those fingers?
Rosa: Correct. Your budget limit is met by this option. There are no other charges in this comparison, and the two-nail art total remains ten dollars.
Ben: That's agreed: both ring fingernails, forty dollars altogether. No extra art today.
Rosa: I have your design approval for that exact option. We will confirm any later change before altering the quantity, placement, or price, rather than adding something without asking.
END REFERENCE
05Responding to discomfort and uncertain nail concernsDo not cover an unexplained concern+

Your goal

Pause a cosmetic request, preserve the client report, and distinguish healthcare referral from salon service approval.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Do not cover an unexplained concern

Before any service starts, client Elena reports unexplained tenderness beside one nail. Elena wants technician Dev to cover the area with product for an event. Dev pauses that request and recommends assessment by an appropriate healthcare professional without naming a condition or suggesting treatment. Elena agrees to seek appropriate advice. The salon must separately assess whether a cosmetic service is suitable. No product has been applied, no cause is established, and the healthcare referral does not guarantee that the salon will approve the requested service.

Elena: Client / Dev: Nail technician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Elena report?

Answer and explanation

C. Unexplained tenderness beside one nail

The client reports tenderness before any service, with no established cause.

2. What happens to the covering request?

Answer and explanation

D. It is paused

Dev pauses the cosmetic request rather than covering an unexplained concern.

3. What does referral establish?

Answer and explanation

C. A route to appropriate health assessment, not service approval

Referral addresses the health concern while cosmetic-service suitability remains a separate decision.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Tenderness is the reported concern, not a diagnosis. It was present before any service began. The request to cover it is paused, with no product applied. Refer the health question without naming a cause or recommending treatment.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
tenderness
Reported soreness or sensitivity, particularly with touch or pressure.report tenderness
nail fold
Skin bordering the nail plate.identify the nail-fold area
adjacent skin
Skin beside the nail or another specified area.describe adjacent skin
redness
Visible red appearance, if actually observed or reported.record reported redness
swelling
Enlargement or puffiness, if actually observed or reported.describe reported swelling
irritation
Uncomfortable response that should not be assigned a cause without assessment.report possible irritation
allergic reaction
Health response requiring appropriate assessment, not a salon guess.refer a suspected allergic reaction
sensitivity
Reported heightened response to touch or a substance.clarify reported sensitivity
unexplained concern
Concern whose cause has not been established.acknowledge an unexplained concern
symptom description
Client's words about what they experience.preserve the symptom description
affected area
Specific location involved in the reported concern.identify the affected area
onset
Time when the reported concern began.clarify reported onset
pre-existing concern
Concern present before the proposed service begins.record a pre-existing concern
cosmetic request
Request to alter appearance, distinct from healthcare treatment.pause the cosmetic request
covering product
Product proposed to conceal an area or change its appearance.decline to apply a covering product
service pause
Temporary stop pending the appropriate next steps.maintain the service pause
healthcare professional
Appropriately qualified person who assesses health concerns.refer to a healthcare professional
referral
Direction to an appropriate professional or service for further assessment.explain the referral
diagnosis
Identification of a health condition through appropriate clinical assessment.avoid giving a diagnosis
treatment advice
Recommendation intended to address a health condition.avoid unsupported treatment advice
cosmetic suitability
Whether the proposed cosmetic service is appropriate after assessment.assess cosmetic suitability
service approval
Salon decision authorizing a specific service under its process.distinguish service approval
event deadline
Client's preferred timing because of an upcoming event.acknowledge the event deadline
factual note
Record limited to the reported or observed facts and agreed actions.make a factual note

Healthcare assessment and salon service approval answer different questions. A referral does not guarantee that a cosmetic service will be suitable or approved afterward. Keep the event deadline, health concern, and unapproved service request distinct.

C Notice the language

A clear boundary can still be kind

Acknowledge why the event matters without making appearance more important than an unexplained concern. Use the client's own description, state what you cannot establish, and explain the next step calmly. A referral should not sound like either a diagnosis or a promise of later approval.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the concern
Thank you for telling me about the tenderness.
Clarify the location
You are describing the area beside this nail.
State the timing
This was reported before any service began.
Pause the request
I will pause the request to cover that area with product.
Avoid a diagnosis
I cannot determine what is causing the tenderness.
Refer appropriately
Please have the concern assessed by an appropriate healthcare professional.
Avoid treatment advice
I cannot recommend a treatment for an unexplained health concern.
Acknowledge the event
I understand why you wanted a cosmetic result for the event.
Keep the boundary
The event does not establish that applying product is suitable.
State what has not happened
No product has been applied.
Separate the decisions
The salon must assess cosmetic suitability separately.
Avoid a future guarantee
The referral does not guarantee service approval afterward.
Preserve the client's words
I will record tenderness beside one nail, without adding a condition name.
Confirm the next step
You will seek appropriate healthcare advice about the concern.
Keep the request pending
The cosmetic request remains paused.
Close factually
No cause is established and no covering service is approved.
6 language and precision notes
Beside versus on
Beside locates the concern next to the nail rather than automatically on the nail plate.
Reported versus diagnosed
A client report supplies information but does not identify a medical condition.
Before
The sequence prevents the record from incorrectly attributing the concern to today's service.
Pause versus treat
Pausing a request does not provide treatment for the underlying concern.
Referral versus clearance
Referral seeks appropriate assessment and does not itself approve a cosmetic service.
Event pressure
A deadline explains the client's preference but does not replace suitability assessment.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which note stays factual?

Answer and explanation

C. Tenderness beside one nail reported before service; covering request paused.

The correct note preserves location, timing, and action without diagnosis or invented causation.

2. Which response overpromises?

Answer and explanation

B. After the referral, we will definitely perform the requested service.

Referral does not guarantee that the salon will later approve cosmetic work.

3. Which next step addresses the health question?

Answer and explanation

B. Assessment by an appropriate healthcare professional

The unexplained health concern needs appropriate assessment rather than cosmetic concealment.

4. Which distinction matters?

Answer and explanation

B. Healthcare assessment and cosmetic-service suitability are separate decisions.

The salon must separately assess the cosmetic service instead of treating referral as approval.

E Conversations

Do not cover an unexplained concern

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Elena: Client / Dev: Nail technician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Cosmetic suitability
  • healthcare professional
  • service approval
  • diagnosis
  • referral
  • factual note
  • event deadline
  • tenderness
  • pause
  • pre-existing concern
  1. Elena
    Before we start, I should mention that the area beside this nail is tender. Could you cover it with product so it looks better for my event?
  2. Dev
    Thank you for telling me. I will 1 that request for now. The tenderness needs appropriate attention before we make assumptions about a cosmetic service.
  3. Elena
    I do not know what caused it. I was hoping the appointment could still go ahead because the event is important to me.
  4. Dev
    I understand the 2. It matters to your plans, but it does not tell us whether applying product to the area is suitable.
  5. Elena
    It's beside this nail, not all over my hand. Can you see where I'm pointing?
  6. Dev
    You are reporting 3 beside this nail. I will keep that description rather than add a condition name or assume a cause.
  7. Elena
    Yes. It was there before I arrived, and nothing from today's appointment has been put on it.
  8. Dev
    I will record it as a 4. No product has been applied today, and no service has started.
  9. Elena
    Who should I ask about it? I haven't had it checked, and I don't know what's causing it.
  10. Dev
    Please have the concern assessed by an appropriate 5. I cannot determine the cause or recommend treatment from this salon conversation.
  11. Elena
    If I get advice, can I come back for the service afterwards?
  12. Dev
    No. The 6 does not guarantee that. The salon must separately decide whether the proposed cosmetic service is suitable through its normal process.
  13. Elena
    So I need advice about the soreness first, and then we'd discuss whether the nail service can go ahead.
  14. Dev
    Exactly. 7 is not established simply by making a referral. We should not promise approval before the relevant decisions have been made.
  15. Elena
    Please just note what I told you. Nobody has diagnosed it yet.
  16. Dev
    I will make a 8 with your description, the fact that it was reported before service, and the request to cover it being paused.
  17. Elena
    That is accurate. I will seek appropriate healthcare advice, and I understand that you are not suggesting a treatment here.
  18. Dev
    Correct. I am not giving a 9 or treatment advice. The immediate salon decision is not to apply a covering product to this unexplained concern.
  19. Elena
    Okay, I won't ask you to cover it. I'll get advice about the tenderness first.
  20. Dev
    The 10 remains unresolved. No cause is established, no product has been applied, and the cosmetic request stays paused while you seek the appropriate advice.

Answers and explanations
  1. pause Turn 2. Pause means stopping the covering request before product is applied, rather than carrying on because the client has an event.
  2. event deadline Turn 4. Event deadline explains why Elena wants a cosmetic result but does not establish that applying a product is suitable.
  3. tenderness Turn 6. Tenderness is Elena's own description of the sensation beside one nail, not a diagnosis of its cause.
  4. pre-existing concern Turn 8. Pre-existing concern means it was present before the proposed service, preventing an inaccurate claim that today's product application caused it.
  5. healthcare professional Turn 10. Healthcare professional identifies the appropriate route for assessing an unexplained health concern, rather than asking the nail technician to diagnose or treat it.
  6. referral Turn 12. Referral directs Elena toward appropriate health assessment; it does not itself approve the proposed cosmetic service or guarantee a later appointment.
  7. Cosmetic suitability Turn 14. Cosmetic suitability is the separate salon assessment of the proposed appearance service, not a substitute for healthcare assessment.
  8. factual note Turn 16. Factual note records the reported tenderness, its timing, the paused request, and the referral without inventing a diagnosis.
  9. diagnosis Turn 18. Diagnosis identifies a health condition through appropriate clinical assessment, which the technician has not performed or claimed.
  10. service approval Turn 20. Service approval has not been given: the covering request remains paused even though the client agrees to seek healthcare advice.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Elena: Before we start, I should mention that the area beside this nail is tender. Could you cover it with product so it looks better for my event?
  2. Dev: Thank you for telling me. I will pause that request for now. The tenderness needs appropriate attention before we make assumptions about a cosmetic service.
  3. Elena: I do not know what caused it. I was hoping the appointment could still go ahead because the event is important to me.
  4. Dev: I understand the event deadline. It matters to your plans, but it does not tell us whether applying product to the area is suitable.
  5. Elena: It's beside this nail, not all over my hand. Can you see where I'm pointing?
  6. Dev: You are reporting tenderness beside this nail. I will keep that description rather than add a condition name or assume a cause.
  7. Elena: Yes. It was there before I arrived, and nothing from today's appointment has been put on it.
  8. Dev: I will record it as a pre-existing concern. No product has been applied today, and no service has started.
  9. Elena: Who should I ask about it? I haven't had it checked, and I don't know what's causing it.
  10. Dev: Please have the concern assessed by an appropriate healthcare professional. I cannot determine the cause or recommend treatment from this salon conversation.
  11. Elena: If I get advice, can I come back for the service afterwards?
  12. Dev: No. The referral does not guarantee that. The salon must separately decide whether the proposed cosmetic service is suitable through its normal process.
  13. Elena: So I need advice about the soreness first, and then we'd discuss whether the nail service can go ahead.
  14. Dev: Exactly. Cosmetic suitability is not established simply by making a referral. We should not promise approval before the relevant decisions have been made.
  15. Elena: Please just note what I told you. Nobody has diagnosed it yet.
  16. Dev: I will make a factual note with your description, the fact that it was reported before service, and the request to cover it being paused.
  17. Elena: That is accurate. I will seek appropriate healthcare advice, and I understand that you are not suggesting a treatment here.
  18. Dev: Correct. I am not giving a diagnosis or treatment advice. The immediate salon decision is not to apply a covering product to this unexplained concern.

Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An event does not answer a suitability question

A client raises tenderness before a nail service.

  1. Technician: You mentioned tenderness beside this nail. Has a healthcare professional assessed it before today's appointment?
  2. Client: No, but I have an event tonight. Could you just cover it so it looks finished?
  3. Technician: I understand the appearance goal, but I can't confirm that covering it is appropriate.
  4. Client: It's only one area. Does that mean you know what's causing the soreness?
  5. Technician: No. A healthcare professional should assess that health concern; I can't diagnose its cause.
  6. Client: Then please don't put a diagnosis in the note. I don't have one.
  7. Technician: I'll record your words and pause this request. The salon still needs to assess service suitability.
  8. Client: Could you explain that to reception without saying I've cancelled every future appointment?
  9. Technician: Yes. A health referral doesn't approve a service or change your future bookings.
  10. Client: Thank you. I want the issue taken seriously without the record saying more than we know.
The client names the wrong area

A technician helps a client locate a reported concern.

  1. Technician: When you say the whole cuticle hurts, could you point to the exact area you mean?
  2. Client: I mean the skin beside the nail, not the hard part on top.
  3. Technician: Thank you. The hard visible part is the nail plate; your concern is beside it.
  4. Client: I don't know the correct term for that skin. I just know it feels sore.
  5. Technician: Your description is enough to report the concern accurately without choosing a diagnosis.
  6. Client: Does asking you about it mean you can tell me what treatment it needs?
  7. Technician: No. A medical question needs an appropriate healthcare professional, not a cosmetic description alone.
  8. Client: Please keep today's service paused while I decide how to get that concern assessed.
  9. Technician: Of course. I'll say you reported soreness beside the nail before any service started.
  10. Client: That's accurate. I appreciate not having my uncertain terminology turned into a medical conclusion.
A lifting report without a cause

A client asks about separation at the edge of a coating.

  1. Technician: You said the coating seems raised at an edge. Which nail are you referring to?
  2. Client: The right index finger. I noticed it this morning, but I don't know why it happened.
  3. Technician: That may describe lifting, meaning separation from the natural nail; it doesn't tell us the cause.
  4. Client: Could the photograph I sent prove it was caused by the last appointment here?
  5. Technician: The photograph can show your concern, but it doesn't establish the cause by itself.
  6. Client: I see. I want a review, not a claim recorded as though we've proved it.
  7. Technician: I'll arrange that and note when you first noticed the change on that nail.
  8. Client: Should I assume a repair is booked just because I sent the photograph this morning?
  9. Technician: No. The review is the agreed next step; the suitable response hasn't been determined.
  10. Client: Then please confirm the review time separately and keep the possible repair unconfirmed for now.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Do not cover an unexplained concern
  1. Read the opening with a calm, firm pause before any product is applied.
  2. Switch roles and read the referral exchange. Do not substitute an invented diagnosis for tenderness.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Separate referral from service approval

Before service, a client reports unexplained soreness beside one nail. No product is applied. The covering request is paused, and the client is directed to an appropriate healthcare professional. Salon suitability remains unassessed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Technician: "The reported concern is ___ beside one nail."

Answer and explanation

A. soreness

Soreness is the supplied client description, not an inferred condition.

2. Client: "No product has been ___."

Answer and explanation

A. applied

The scenario explicitly keeps the cosmetic application from starting.

3. Technician: "The covering request remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. paused

Referral does not restart or authorize the cosmetic service.

4. Reception: "Cosmetic suitability is still ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unassessed

The salon must assess suitability separately from the healthcare referral.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Course: Nail Technician English
Professional audience: Nail technicians, manicurists, pedicurists, and nail-salon reception staff

This book teaches workplace English, not nail procedures, product removal, disinfection methods, medical diagnosis, treatment, or legal requirements. Follow professional training, current product instructions, actual salon procedures, applicable local rules, and emergency arrangements. Health concerns belong with an appropriate healthcare professional; cosmetic-service suitability requires its own salon assessment. No example authorizes covering an unexplained concern with product.

Language guidance: Tenderness is the reported concern, not a diagnosis. It was present before any service began. The request to cover it is paused, with no product applied. Refer the health question without naming a cause or recommending treatment.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Thank you for telling me about the tenderness. / You are describing the area beside this nail. / This was reported before any service began. / I will pause the request to cover that area with product. / I cannot determine what is causing the tenderness. / Please have the concern assessed by an appropriate healthcare professional. / I cannot recommend a treatment for an unexplained health concern. / I understand why you wanted a cosmetic result for the event. / The event does not establish that applying product is suitable. / No product has been applied. / The salon must assess cosmetic suitability separately. / The referral does not guarantee service approval afterward. / I will record tenderness beside one nail, without adding a condition name. / You will seek appropriate healthcare advice about the concern. / The cosmetic request remains paused. / No cause is established and no covering service is approved.
Vocabulary: tenderness: Reported soreness or sensitivity, particularly with touch or pressure. / nail fold: Skin bordering the nail plate. / adjacent skin: Skin beside the nail or another specified area. / redness: Visible red appearance, if actually observed or reported. / swelling: Enlargement or puffiness, if actually observed or reported. / irritation: Uncomfortable response that should not be assigned a cause without assessment. / allergic reaction: Health response requiring appropriate assessment, not a salon guess. / sensitivity: Reported heightened response to touch or a substance. / unexplained concern: Concern whose cause has not been established. / symptom description: Client's words about what they experience. / affected area: Specific location involved in the reported concern. / onset: Time when the reported concern began. / pre-existing concern: Concern present before the proposed service begins. / cosmetic request: Request to alter appearance, distinct from healthcare treatment. / covering product: Product proposed to conceal an area or change its appearance. / service pause: Temporary stop pending the appropriate next steps. / healthcare professional: Appropriately qualified person who assesses health concerns. / referral: Direction to an appropriate professional or service for further assessment. / diagnosis: Identification of a health condition through appropriate clinical assessment. / treatment advice: Recommendation intended to address a health condition. / cosmetic suitability: Whether the proposed cosmetic service is appropriate after assessment. / service approval: Salon decision authorizing a specific service under its process. / event deadline: Client's preferred timing because of an upcoming event. / factual note: Record limited to the reported or observed facts and agreed actions.
Speaking task: Read the opening with a calm, firm pause before any product is applied. Switch roles and read the referral exchange. Do not substitute an invented diagnosis for tenderness. Complete and check the transfer; preserve the separate health assessment and unassessed cosmetic suitability.
Partner: Elena: Client / Dev: Nail technician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Before service, a client reports unexplained soreness beside one nail. No product is applied. The covering request is paused, and the client is directed to an appropriate healthcare professional. Salon suitability remains unassessed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Elena: That is accurate. I will seek appropriate healthcare advice, and I understand that you are not suggesting a treatment here.
Dev: Correct. I am not giving a diagnosis or treatment advice. The immediate salon decision is not to apply a covering product to this unexplained concern.
Elena: Okay, I won't ask you to cover it. I'll get advice about the tenderness first.
Dev: The service approval remains unresolved. No cause is established, no product has been applied, and the cosmetic request stays paused while you seek the appropriate advice.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

06Giving a clear station-readiness updateTidy is not confirmed ready+

Your goal

Distinguish visible tidiness from completed readiness checks and offer a verified alternative without inventing a release time.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Tidy is not confirmed ready

Receptionist Maya needs a station for a waiting client. Technician Owen reports that station three looks tidy but its required readiness checks are incomplete. Responsible technician Rosa has confirmed station two ready. Owen offers station two and keeps station three marked pending until its actual checks are completed and readiness is confirmed through the salon process. No completion time for station three is known. This is a communication task, not a set of cleaning, disinfection, equipment, or chemical-use instructions.

Maya: Receptionist / Owen: Nail technician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which station is confirmed ready?

Answer and explanation

C. Station two

Rosa has confirmed station two ready through the relevant salon process.

2. What is station three's status?

Answer and explanation

B. Tidy, with required checks incomplete

Appearance and readiness differ; station three still has incomplete required checks.

3. What time will station three be ready?

Answer and explanation

C. No completion time is known

The case supplies no verified completion time for the pending station.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Station three is tidy but not confirmed ready because required checks are incomplete. Station two is confirmed ready by Rosa. Offer the verified alternative without silently upgrading station three or inventing a completion time.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
station
Designated work area for a client service.identify the station
readiness check
Required review before a station is confirmed available for use.complete a readiness check
confirmed ready
Status supported by the responsible person's completed process.report confirmed-ready status
pending status
State of waiting for an unfinished check or decision.keep the pending status
tidiness
Visible orderliness, not proof of completed hygiene or readiness checks.distinguish tidiness from readiness
cleaning
Removal of dirt or material, distinct from every further required process.distinguish cleaning from disinfection
disinfection
Process intended to control specified microorganisms under applicable instructions.refer to disinfection requirements
single-use item
Item intended for one use under its instructions.identify a single-use item
reusable implement
Tool intended for repeated use subject to the required processing.identify a reusable implement
service tray
Surface or container holding items for a service.identify the service tray
work surface
Surface used during the service.check work-surface status
clean storage
Designated storage for appropriately prepared items.identify clean storage
used-item area
Designated place for items awaiting the required handling.identify the used-item area
waste container
Receptacle for waste under the applicable procedure.check the waste container
product label
Product information including identity, instructions, and warnings.consult the product label
safety data sheet
Document describing a product's hazards and relevant safety information.locate the safety data sheet
ventilation
Movement or exchange of air through the relevant workplace system.report a ventilation concern
local exhaust
System capturing airborne material near its source.identify local exhaust
personal protective equipment
Equipment used to reduce exposure to workplace hazards.confirm required personal protective equipment
check record
Record showing what has been checked and its status.update the check record
responsible technician
Person accountable for the relevant station confirmation.name the responsible technician
release confirmation
Statement that the station has met the required readiness process.await release confirmation
sterilization
Validated process eliminating viable microorganisms including bacterial spores; not a synonym for storage.distinguish disinfection from sterilization
cross-contamination
Transfer of unwanted microorganisms or material between tools, surfaces, or people.prevent cross-contamination

Cleaning, disinfection, storage, product information, and equipment terms describe different parts of real workplace systems. This lesson does not supply procedures or authorize a shortcut. Use actual instructions, training, and the responsible confirmation process.

C Notice the language

Use status words colleagues can act on

Tidy, nearly done, and should be fine may sound reassuring while leaving an essential check unclear. Give the specific station, verified status, and available next step. A waiting client creates a scheduling need, not evidence that unfinished checks can be treated as complete.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the station
Station three is the one still awaiting checks.
Describe only what is known
It looks tidy, but its required checks are incomplete.
Avoid an appearance shortcut
Tidiness does not establish confirmed readiness.
State the verified alternative
Station two has been confirmed ready.
Name the confirmation source
Rosa is the responsible technician who confirmed it.
Offer a workable next step
We can offer the client station two.
Keep the pending status
Station three should remain marked pending.
Avoid a guessed time
I do not have a confirmed completion time for station three.
Acknowledge the wait
I know the client is waiting; we have a verified alternative.
Ask a precise question
Which station has completed the required readiness process?
Keep procedures separate
The checks must follow the actual salon process.
Prevent an inaccurate update
Please do not change three to ready because the surface looks clear.
Read back the numbers
Two is confirmed ready; three is pending.
Confirm the next update
We will update three after the required confirmation.
Give a client-facing message
A confirmed-ready station is available for you.
Close the handover
Use the verified status, not an estimate based on appearance.
6 language and precision notes
Tidy versus ready
Visible order does not prove that all required checks are complete.
Two versus three
Read back the station numbers to prevent a correct status attaching to the wrong place.
Confirmed by
Names the responsible source rather than implying personal observation of every check.
Pending
This means unfinished, not failed forever or ready for immediate use.
Available alternative
An actual ready station gives a useful option without pressuring an incomplete one.
No completion time
An unknown time should remain unknown until a reliable update is available.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handover is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Two confirmed ready by Rosa; three tidy with checks incomplete.

The correct message preserves both station numbers and their different verified statuses.

2. Which message should reception give the client?

Answer and explanation

B. A confirmed-ready station is available at station two.

Station two provides the verified option without inventing readiness for station three.

3. What would justify updating station three's status?

Answer and explanation

A. Completion of its required checks and the actual readiness confirmation

Readiness must follow the required process rather than appearance or scheduling pressure.

4. Which question is most precise?

Answer and explanation

C. Has station three received the required readiness confirmation?

The question asks about the specific unresolved status rather than a vague impression.
